\order{v^6}

\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,0)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,13,0)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,14,0)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,-4,1)}(e)\\
\mathrm{J}_{(p,9,1)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,10,1)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,2)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,5,2)}(e)\\
\mathrm{J}_{(p,6,2)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,3)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,3)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,3)}(e)\\
\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,3)}(e)&\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,3)}(e)&\mathrm{K}_{(p,1,0)}(e)&\mathrm{K}_{(p,2,0)}(e)\\
\mathrm{K}_{(p,3,0)}(e)&\mathrm{K}_{(p,4,0)}(e)&\mathrm{K}_{(p,5,0)}(e)&\mathrm{K}_{(p,6,0)}(e)\end{array}

It is precisely the presence of such integrals that has long forced frequency-domain eccentric waveforms to rely on small-eccentricity expansion.
Such expansion can be easily evaluated.
We found
43
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}(e)=\bigg(\frac{e}{2}\bigg)^{p}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{j}_{(p,a,b,n)}\bigg(\frac{e}{2}\bigg)^{2n},

(22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}(e)=\bigg(\frac{e}{2}\bigg)^{p}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{k}_{(p,a,n)}\bigg(\frac{e}{2}\bigg)^{2n},

(23)

where

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{(p,a,0,n)}=\frac{p^{p}}{n!(p+n)!}\sum_{k=0}^{n}p^{2k}(-1)^{k}a_{(2n-2k)}\binom{n}{k}\sum_{m=0}^{p}p^{-m}\big(a+2(n-k)\big)_{(m)}\binom{p}{m},

(24)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{(p,a,1,n)}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{2m-1+p}\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1+p-k}\frac{p^{j}(k+2n-2m)!a_{(2m-1-j-k+p)}}{(n-m)!j!(2m-1-j-k+p)!(k+n-m+1)!}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\min(j,m)}(-1)^{j^{\prime}}\binom{j}{j^{\prime}}\bigg[\binom{2m+p-1-k-j}{m-1-k-j^{\prime}}-\binom{2m+p-1-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}}\bigg],

(25)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{(p,a,2,n)}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2m-1+p}\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1+p-k}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\frac{(k+1)p^{j}(k+2n-2m)!a_{(2m-1-j-k+p)}}{k^{\prime}(k-k^{\prime}+1)(n-m)!j!(2m-1-j-k+p)!(k+n-m+1)!}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\min(j,m)}(-1)^{j^{\prime}}\binom{j}{j^{\prime}}\bigg[\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-k-j^{\prime}}-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-k-j^{\prime}+k^{\prime}}+\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}}\bigg]

(26)

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{(p,a,3,n)}=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{2m-1+p}\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1+p-k}\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{k}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}=1}^{k-k^{\prime}}\frac{(k+1)p^{j}(k+2n-2m)!a_{(2m-1-j-k+p)}}{k^{\prime}k^{\prime\prime}(k-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}+1)(n-m)!j!(2m-1-j-k+p)!(k+n-m+1)!}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\min(j,m)}(-1)^{j^{\prime}}\binom{j}{j^{\prime}}\bigg[\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-k-j^{\prime}}+\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-j^{\prime}+k+k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}}-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-j^{\prime}-k+k^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-1-j^{\prime}-k+k^{\prime}}+\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}}+\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}}-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}-k^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\binom{2m-1+p-k-j}{m-j^{\prime}}\bigg],

(27)

\displaystyle\hat{k}_{(p,a,n)}=\sum_{k=0}^{2n+p-1}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k}\sum_{k^{\prime\prime}=0}^{k-k^{\prime}}\frac{p^{k-k^{\prime}}(-1)^{k^{\prime\prime}+1}a_{(2n+p-1-k)}}{(k-k^{\prime})!(2n+p-1-k)!}\binom{k-k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime\prime}}\binom{2n+p-k+k^{\prime}}{n-k^{\prime\prime}},

(28)

where
a_{(b)}:=\Gamma(a+b)/\Gamma(a)
denotes upper factorial or Pochhammer symbol.

However, for large eccentricities the required expansion order becomes extremely high.
Moreover, to achieve waveform accuracy comparable to that of circular orbit cases, one must include a much larger number of Fourier modes
50
.
If one could instead find simple and accurate analytic approximations to these integrals, the speed and accuracy of frequency-domain eccentric waveform calculations could be substantially improved.
This, however, requires a sufficiently detailed understanding of their mathematical structure when
e\to 1
and
p\to\infty
.

In the following section, we will present two methods to derive the asymptotic expansions of these integrals in the high-eccentricity regime.
The first method is more direct, providing a large-eccentricity asymptotic expansion at fixed
p
.
The second method is the uniform asymptotic expansion that applies in the limit where
p\to\infty
.

These two methods can be cross-checked against each other. We will also demonstrate their distinct roles in gravitational-wave calculations in section
IV
and section
V
.

III
Large eccentricity asymptotic expansion of PN-elliptic integrals

In this section, we present a detailed account of the evaluation procedures for the two asymptotic methods.
In Section
III.1
, we derive the asymptotic expansion in the limit
e\to 1
at fixed
p
.
In Section
III.2
, we introduce the uniform asymptotic expansion valid in the joint limit
p\to\infty
and
e\to 1
.
Finally, in Section
III.3
, we perform a cross-check between the two methods.

III.1
Asymptotic approximation

Inspecting these two types of integrals (
20
) and (
21
), it is clear that when
a\leq 0
and
b=0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,0)}=-\frac{e}{p}J^{\prime}_{p}(ep),

(29)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,0)}=-\frac{2}{p^{2}}J_{p}(ep).

(30)

Due to the recurrence relations given below, it is not necessary to compute all cases with
a<0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)}=-\frac{\Delta_{e}}{p}J_{p}(ep)-\frac{1}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,0)}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,0)}-\frac{e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}+\frac{e}{\Delta_{e}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,0)},

(31)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,1)}=\frac{1}{p}\bigg(1+\frac{2}{\Delta_{e}}\bigg)\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,0)}+\frac{2}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}-\frac{2}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}-\frac{2e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)},

(32)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-4,1)}=\frac{1}{2p}\bigg(2+\frac{3}{\Delta_{e}}\bigg)\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,0)}-\frac{\Delta_{e}}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,0)}+\frac{6}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)}-\frac{6}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}-\frac{3e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)},

(33)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,2)}=\frac{2}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}-\frac{2}{p^{2}\Delta_{e}^{2}}J_{p}(ep)-\frac{2\Delta_{e}}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}-\frac{2}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,1)}+\frac{2}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,1)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,2)}-\frac{2e}{\Delta_{e}p}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,1)},

(34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,2)}=\frac{1}{p}\bigg(2+\frac{4}{\Delta_{e}}\bigg)\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)}-\frac{4}{\Delta_{e}p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,0)}-\frac{2\Delta_{e}}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}+\frac{2}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,2)}+\frac{4}{p^{2}}J_{p}(ep)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{2}{p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,2)}-\frac{2e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,2)},

(35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,3)}=\frac{3}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,2)}-\frac{6}{\Delta_{e}^{2}p^{2}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}-\frac{3\Delta_{e}}{p}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,2)}+\frac{6}{\Delta_{e}^{2}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,0)}-\frac{3}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,2)}-\frac{6}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,0)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{3}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,2)}-\frac{e}{p^{2}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,3)}+\frac{3e}{\Delta_{e}p^{3}}\partial_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,2)}.

(36)

When
a>0
, however, the integrand develops singular behavior in the limits
z\to 0
and
\Delta_{e}\to 0
, leading to a divergence of the integral.
Moreover, when
b>0
, even if
a<0
, a direct expansion of the integrand gives rise to higher-order derivatives of
\delta_{\chi}
, which also introduce singular behavior into the integrand.
The problem must therefore be treated as an asymptotic analysis of a parameter-dependent integral
20
;
53
.
Noting that the leading divergence of the denominator is governed by
(1-e\cos{z})^{-1}\sim(z^{2}+\Delta_{e}^{2})^{-1}=\Delta_{e}^{-2}\big((z/\Delta_{e})^{2}+1\big)
, it is natural to introduce the new variable
t=z/\Delta_{e}
.
In this way, the leading-order divergence

\int(1-e\cos{z})^{-a}\differential{z}\sim\Delta_{e}^{1-2a}\int(t^{2}+1)^{-a}\differential{t}

can be factored out explicitly, leaving a regular remainder.

In general, consider the integral

\displaystyle I=\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{f(\Delta_{e},z)}{(1-e\cos{z})^{a}}\differential{z}.

(37)

Here the function
f(\Delta_{e},z)
is analytic over the full domain
0\leq\Delta_{e}\leq 1
and
0\leq z\leq\pi
. When
a>0
, this integral may diverge in the limit
\Delta_{e}\to 0
.
After introducing the change of variables
t=z/\Delta_{e}
, the divergent part can be extracted explicitly.
As a result, the
\Delta_{e}
-expansion of the integrand becomes uniformly convergent, and one obtains

\displaystyle I=\Delta_{e}^{1-2a}\left(\int_{0}^{\pi/\Delta_{e}}\frac{2g(t)}{(t^{2}+1)^{a}}\differential{t}+\order{\Delta_e}\right),

(38)

where

\displaystyle g(t):=\lim_{\Delta_{e}\to 0}f(\Delta_{e},\Delta_{e}t).

(39)

However, it would be incorrect to proceed by directly performing a small-
\Delta_{e}
asymptotic expansion of (
38
).
The reason is that this expression captures only the local behavior near
z\to 0
, while the contribution from the region near
z\to\pi
is lost.
The problem is therefore, in essence, a multiscale matching problem; see, for example, the textbook discussions in
36
;
14
.

The correct procedure is to split the integration domain into two regions,

\int_{0}^{\pi}\Rightarrow\int_{0}^{\epsilon}+\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi}
,
where
\epsilon\ll 1
is a small constant.
For the interval
(0,\epsilon)
, one may follow the previous steps, except that the upper limit in (
38
) is replaced by
\epsilon/\Delta_{e}
, and the resulting integrals are then expanded in the limit
\Delta_{e}\to 0
.
For the interval
(\epsilon,\pi)
, the integrand is uniformly convergent, so it can be expanded directly into a series.
Since the sum of these two parts must be independent of the arbitrary constant
\epsilon
, expanding each part in powers of
\epsilon
must lead to a cancellation of all
\epsilon
-dependent terms at any given order in
\Delta_{e}
.

Meanwhile, there is another approach.
Since we only need the expansion up to finite order—in fact, in this section we only retain terms up to
\Delta_{e}^{4}
, more precisely, it is
\Delta_{e}^{\alpha+4}
, where
\alpha
is the leading asymptotic power.
It is sufficient to construct a suitable matching function
g_{\mathrm{match}}(e,z)
whose divergent asymptotic behavior in the joint limit
z\to 0
,
\Delta_{e}\to 0
agrees, through order
\order{\Delta_e^4}
, with that of the integrands in (
20
) and (
21
).
One may then decompose the integral as

\displaystyle I=\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(\frac{f(e,z)}{(1-e\cos{z})^{a}}-g_{\mathrm{match}}(e,z)\bigg)\differential{z}+\int_{0}^{\pi}g_{\mathrm{match}}(e,z)\differential{z}.

(40)

The first term may then be treated as approximately uniformly convergent up to order
\Delta_{e}^{4}
, and can therefore be expanded straightforwardly into a series.

In the following subsections, we will apply these two methods respectively to
b=0
and
b\neq 0
cases respectively.

III.1.1
b=0
case

Let’s start from the simplest case
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}
.
We found it is sufficient to choose

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{match}}(e,z)=\frac{1}{(1-e\cos{z})^{a}},

(41)

which already yields the expansion up to order
\Delta_{e}^{4}
.
This integral can be evaluated by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1}{(1-e\cos{z})^{n}}\differential{z}=\Delta_{e}^{-n}P_{n-1}(\Delta_{e}^{-1}),

(42)

where
P_{n}(x)
is the
n
th order Legendre polynomial.
We obtain the following expansion

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,0)}=1+\frac{\Delta_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-\sin{z}) - 1}}{1-\cos{z}}\differential{z}+\frac{\Delta^{3}_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1}{2(1-\cos{z})}\bigg[\frac{\cos{z}\big(1-\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}\big)}{1-\cos{z}}

\displaystyle\qquad-p\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\bigg]\differential{z}+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(43)

\displaystyle\Delta^{3}_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,0)}=1+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-e\sin{z}) - 1}}{(1-\cos{z})^{2}}\differential{z}+\order{\Delta_e^5}.

(44)

One can verify, using trigonometric identities, that these two integrals are actually equal to each other,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-e\sin{z}) - 1}}{(1-\cos{z})^{2}}\differential{z}\equiv\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1}{2(1-\cos{z})}\bigg[\frac{\cos{z}\big(1-\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}\big)}{1-\cos{z}}-p\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\bigg]\differential{z},

(45)

Nevertheless, we retain their original forms for clarity, and also to facilitate comparison with the subsequent discussion of the expansion of
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
.

When
a>2
, one has
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}=\Delta_{e}^{-a}P_{a-1}(1/\Delta_{e})+\order{\Delta_e^{6-2a}}
.
This implies that the complicated coefficients—those that can only be expressed in terms of integrals—do not appear at lower orders, and the overall structure is therefore significantly simplified.

The advantage of choosing this form is that, using the relation

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}=-\partialderivative{a}\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-e\sin{z})}-1}{(1-e\cos{z})^{a}}\ln{(1-e\cos{z})}\differential{z}-\partialderivative{a}\frac{1}{\Delta_{e}^{a}}P_{a-1}\big(\Delta_{e}^{-1}\big),

(46)

one can straightforwardly obtain the expansion of
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
.
The second term is given by

\displaystyle\partialderivative{a}\frac{1}{\Delta_{e}^{a}}P_{a-1}\big(\Delta_{e}^{-1}\big)=-\frac{\ln\Delta_{e}}{\Delta_{e}^{a}}P_{a-1}\big(\Delta_{e}^{-1}\big)+\frac{1}{\Delta_{e}^{a}}\partialderivative{a}P_{a-1}\big(\Delta_{e}^{-1}\big).

(47)

The derivative with respective to the order of Legendre polynomial reads

\displaystyle\partialderivative{n}P_{n}(x)=\ln{\frac{x+1}{2}}P_{n}(x)+2\Delta{H}_{n}^{2n}P_{n}(x)+2\sum_{k=0}^{k-1}\frac{(-1)^{n+k}(2k+1)}{(n-k)(n+k+1)}P_{k}(x),

(48)

where
\Delta{H}^{a}_{b}:=H_{a}-H_{b}
denotes the difference between the
a
th harmonic number
H_{a}
and the
b
th harmonic number
H_{b}
.
One would obtain the expansions

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}\mathrm{K}_{(p,1,0)}=\ln{\big( 2\Delta_e^2 \big)}+\Delta_{e}\bigg(-1+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}-1}{(1-e\cos{z})}\ln{(1-\cos{z})}\differential{z}\bigg)+

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{2}+\Delta_{e}^{3}\Bigg[-\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1}{2(1-\cos{z})}\bigg[\ln{(1-\cos{z})}\bigg(\frac{\cos{z}\big(1-\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}\big)}{1-\cos{z}}-p\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\cos{z}\big(1-\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}\big)}{1-\cos{z}}\bigg]\differential{z}\Bigg]+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{4}}{4}+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(49)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{3}\mathrm{K}_{(p,2,0)}=-1+\ln{\big( 2\Delta_e^2 \big)}+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{2}+\Delta_{e}^{3}\bigg(-\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}-1}{(1-e\cos{z})^{2}}\ln{(1-\cos{z})}\differential{z}\bigg)+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{4}}{4}+\order{\Delta_e^5}.

(50)

Similarly, when
a>2
,
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}=\partial_{a}\Delta_{e}^{-1}P_{a-1}(1/\Delta_{e})
. Now we’ve finished the expansion of all
b=0
cases.
Although some of these coefficients can only be expressed in terms of integrals, we will show in the following sections how to evaluate them.

III.1.2
b\neq 0
case

When
b\neq 0
, the integrals involve structures such as
\delta_{\chi}
that are not amenable to analytic integration.
We therefore directly employ the matched asymptotic approximation method to compute the expansion.
Due to the length of the resulting expressions, we present them in Appendix
A
.

At this point, we have completed the analysis of the asymptotic behavior in the limit
e\to 1
at fixed
p
.
However, from a global perspective this description is incomplete.
We therefore proceed to analyze the asymptotic behavior of these integrals in the limit
p\to\infty
.

III.2
Uniform asymptotic expansion

We now introduce the integral approximation method of uniform asymptotic expansion (UAE).
More details can be found in
20
;
60
;
65
.

Consider the integral

\displaystyle I_{p}(e;F):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}F(e,z)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\phi(e,z)},

(51)

where
\phi(e,z)=z-e\sin{z}
,
F(e,z)
is an analytical function when
|z|<\pi
and
0\leq e<1
.
We decompose the integrand function
F
into the even part and odd part,

\displaystyle F(e,z)=\frac{1}{2}\big(F(e,z)+F(e,-z)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\big(F(e,z)-F(e,-z)\big):=F_{E}(e,z)+F_{O}(e,z).

(52)

The integral
I_{p}(e;F)
is decomposed as

\displaystyle I_{p}(e;F)=I^{(E)}_{p}(e;F)+I^{(O)}_{p}(e;F),

(53)

where

\displaystyle I^{(E)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}F_{E}(e,z)\cos{\big( p\phi(e,z) \big)}\differential{z},

\displaystyle I^{(O)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{i}F_{O}(e,z)\sin{\big( p\phi(e,z) \big)}\differential{z}.

(54)

The phase function
\phi(e,z)
gives two saddle points or stationary phase points, which are the two roots of
\partial_{z}\phi(e,z)=0
, denotes by
z_{\pm}=\pm\arccos{1/e}
.
Since
0\leq e<1
,
z_{\pm}
are complex numbers, therefore we introduce parameter transformation
t=t(z)
such that

\displaystyle\phi(e,z(t))=\frac{t^{3}}{3}+\zeta t,

(55)

the solution in real axis reads

\displaystyle t(z)=\frac{2^{1/3}\zeta}{\big(-3\phi(e,z)+\sqrt{4\zeta^{3}+9\phi(e,z)^{2}}\big)^{1/3}}-\frac{1}{2^{1/3}}\big(-3\phi(e,z)+\sqrt{4\zeta^{3}+9\phi(e,z)^{2}}\big)^{1/3}.

(56)

Therefore
\zeta
is given by

\displaystyle\zeta^{3/2}=\frac{3\mathrm{i}}{4}\big(\phi(e,z_{-})-\phi(e,z_{+})\big)=\frac{3}{2}\big(\ln{\betae^{-1}}-\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\big).

(57)

One can check that
\zeta=\Delta_{e}^{2}/2^{1/3}+\order{\Delta_e^4}
.
The saddle points
t_{\pm}=\pm\mathrm{i}\zeta^{1/2}
. In
60
, the whole process are performed on the complex plane.
Here, we simply transfer the variables to the real axis, but in fact, the two are equivalent.
It shows that when
e\to 1
, the two saddle points
t_{\pm}\to 0
along the imaginary axis.
The oscillating integral (
51
) is mainly contributed by the portion close to the saddle points, so when
e\to 1
, the contribution around
t\to 0
will become increasingly significant. Suppose a scale transformation
s=p^{1/3}t
, the integrals in terms of
s
read

\displaystyle I^{(E)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{p^{1/3}\pi}\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}p^{1/3}}G_{E}(e,s)\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(58)

\displaystyle I^{(O)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{p^{1/3}\pi}\int_{0}^{t_{\pi}p^{1/3}}G_{O}(e,s)\sin{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(59)

where
y:=p^{2/3}\zeta
, and
t_{\pi}:=t(z=\pi)
is the real root of the equation
t_{\pi}^{3}/3+\zeta t_{\pi}=\pi
. We define

\displaystyle G_{E}(e,s):=F_{E}(e,z(s/p^{1/3}))z^{\prime}(s/p^{1/3}),

(60)

\displaystyle G_{O}(e,s):=\mathrm{i}F_{O}(e,z(s/p^{1/3}))z^{\prime}(s/p^{1/3}).

(61)

Since
z^{\prime}(t)=(t^{2}+\zeta)/(1-e\cos{z})
is even,
G_{E},G_{O}
are still even and odd respectively.

The above process is nothing but the UAE for the case of two merging saddle points, as mentioned in
60
.
In this case the phase of the integral is of the type of Airy function
\mathrm{Ai}(y)
and Scorer function
\mathrm{Gi}(y)
, which take the real axis integral forms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ai}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(62)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Gi}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sin{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s}.

(63)

In the limit of
p\to\infty
, the integrals (
58
)-(
59
) can be expressed asymptotically in terms of
\mathrm{Ai}(y)
and
\mathrm{Gi}(y)
. The resulting error arises from the contribution of the tail integral over
s\in(p^{1/3}t_{\pi},\infty)
.
The error of the tail term can be estimated by thinking about a repeated integration by parts: since the oscillatory kernel effectively vanishes as
s\to\infty
, only the boundary term
s=p^{1/3}t_{\pi}
remains.
Moreover, each integration by parts introduces an additional factor of order
s^{-2}\sim p^{-2/3}
.
Because of the structure of the trigonometric function itself, the first boundary term does not appear until after twice integration by parts.
Combined with the scaling of the integrand, this implies that the resulting error is of order
\order{p^{-5/3}}
.
In the subsequent calculations, we will keep terms only up to the order preceding the tail contribution, meaning the tail itself will be omitted.
We have

\displaystyle I^{(E)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{p^{1/3}\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{E}(e,s)\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s}+\order{p^{-5/3}},

(64)

\displaystyle I^{(O)}_{p}(e;F)=\frac{1}{p^{1/3}\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}G_{O}(e,s)\sin{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s}+\order{p^{-5/3}}

(65)

The fundamental framework of UAE is established so far.
Follow this procedure, one can easily reproduce the UAE of Bessel function
J_{p}(ep)
by substituting
F\equiv 1

30

\displaystyle J_{p}(ep)=\left(\frac{4\zeta}{1-e^{2}}\right)^{1/4}p^{-1/3}\mathrm{Ai}\left(p^{2/3}\zeta\right)+\order{p^{-5/3}},

(66)

With these mathematical preparations, we can now begin to discuss how to use UAE to construct a proper approximation for PN-elliptic (
20
) and (
21
).
In the following subsections, we will describe how to compute the UAE for
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}
,
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
, and
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b>0)}
, respectively.

III.2.1
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}

The simplest case is
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}
and we only consider
a>0
.
In this case, the integrand
F(e,z)=F_{E}(e,z)=1/(1-e\cos{z})^{a}
is even.
The UAE procedure is expressing
F_{E}(e,t)
in terms of the following Laurent series
65
;
44
,

\displaystyle F_{E}(e,t)=\sum_{j=-a}^{\infty}a_{j}(t^{2}+\zeta)^{j}.

(67)

Substituting it it into the integral (
65
), one would obtain the integrals that take the form of

\displaystyle V_{j}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}(s^{2}+y)^{j}\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s}.

(68)

With the help of the expansion,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-e\cos{z(t)}}=\frac{h_{0}}{t^{2}+\zeta}+\frac{h_{0}(h_{0}^{3}-2)}{12\zeta}-\frac{h_{0}(20-5h_{0}^{6}+12h_{0}^{2}\zeta)}{384\zeta^{2}}(t^{2}+\zeta)+\order{(t^2+\zeta)^2},

(69)

where
h_{0}:=(4\zeta/\Delta_{e}^{2})^{1/4}
.
This yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}(e)=h_{0}^{a+1}p^{2a/3}\bigg[\frac{1}{p^{1/3}}V_{-a}(p^{2/3}\zeta)-\frac{(a+1)(h_{0}^{3}-2)}{12p\zeta}V_{1-a}(p^{2/3}\zeta)+\order{p^{-5/3}}\bigg],

(70)

We will encounter many integrals of the form
V_{j}(y)
, but these integrals can be expressed in terms of a small set of elementary types.
For the case where
j>0
, one can expand
(s^{2}+y)^{j}
and obtain

\displaystyle V_{j}(y)=\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j}\binom{j}{j^{\prime}}(-1)^{j^{\prime}}y^{j-j^{\prime}}\derivative[2j']{y}\mathrm{Ai}(y).

(71)

For the case
j
is negative, using integration by parts and the property that the oscillation phase vanishes at infinity, we obtain the recurrences,

\displaystyle V_{-(j+1)}=\frac{1}{4yj(j-2)}\big(2(j-2)(2j-1)V_{-j}+V_{-(j-3)}\big),

(72)

\displaystyle\derivative{y}V_{-j}=(j+1)\big(V_{-(j+1)}-2yV_{-(j+2)}\big).

(73)

Obviously,
V_{-1}(y)
diverges when
y\to 0
.
In the end, we obtain all the required UAE of
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,0)}
in terms of
V_{0},V_{-1}
.
Because their explicit forms are rather cumbersome, we present them in the Appendix
B.1
.

III.2.2
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}

There is a logarithmic term
\ln(1-e\cos{z})
in the integral
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
, which is even but also diverges when
e\to 1
and
z\to 0
.
We expand the integrand as the following form

\displaystyle\frac{\ln(1-e\cos{z})}{(1-e\cos{z})^{a}}=\sum_{j=-a}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{1}c_{j,k}(t^{2}+\zeta)^{j}\ln{(t^2+\zeta)}^{k}.

(74)

There will be a new kind of integral

\displaystyle U_{j}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\ln{(s^2+y)}(s^{2}+y)^{j}\cos{(s^3/3+sy)}\differential{s}=\partialderivative{j}V_{j}.

(75)

Similarly, for
j>0
the recurrences

\displaystyle U_{j}(y)=4(j-1)(j-3)yU_{j-4}(y)-2(j-1)(2j-5)U_{j-3}(y)+8(j-2)yV_{j-4}(y)-2(4j-7)V_{j-3}(y),

(76)

\displaystyle U_{-(j+1)}(y)=\frac{1}{4j(j-2)y}\Big(2(j-2)(2j-1)U_{-j}(y)+U_{-(j-3)}(y)+8(j-1)yV_{-(j+1)}(y)+2(5-4j)V_{-j}(y)\Big),

(77)

and the algebraic relations

\displaystyle U_{-2}(y)=-\frac{1}{2y}\Big(U^{\prime}_{0}(y)-U_{-1}(y)+V_{-1}(y)-2yV_{-1}(y)\Big),

(78)

\displaystyle U_{-3}(y)=-\frac{1}{4y^{2}}\Big(U^{\prime}_{0}(y)+U^{\prime}_{-1}(y)-U_{-1}(y)+V_{-1}(y)-yV_{-2}(y)-2y^{2}V_{-3}(y)\Big)

(79)

can replace
U_{j}(y)
with
U_{0}(y),U_{-1}(y)
and their derivatives.

Similarly, one can obtain the UAE of
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
. We show the results in appendix
B.2
.

III.2.3
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}

When
b>0
, it becomes more complicated.
The reason is that
\delta_{\chi}(e=1,z)
is discontinuous at
z=2k\pi
.
When expanding it into the series of
t^{2}+\zeta
according to the steps of UAE described above, one would find the leading order is
\ln(\zeta+t^2)
, and the higher-order coefficients of the expansion diverge at
e\to 1
.
This divergence is precisely because of the discontinuity of
\delta_{\chi}(1,z)
, or rather, because
\partial_{z}\delta_{\chi}(e,z)
diverges at
e\to 1
.
Therefore, the correct approach is to first subtract this part that leads to singularities. We denote the singular part as

\displaystyle\delta^{\mathrm{sing}}_{\chi}(e,z)=2\arctan\big(z/\tilde\zeta\big).

(80)

where
\tilde{\zeta}:=\ln 1/\beta_{e}=(2/3)\zeta^{3/2}+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}
.
The rest part
\delta_{\chi}(e,z)-\delta^{\mathrm{sing}}_{\chi}(e,z)
is regular and can be processed normally by the UAE method.
However,
\delta^{\mathrm{sing}}_{\chi}(e,z)
does not have similar expansion structure, so we need to handle it separately.

We expand
z(t)
around
z_{\pm}

\displaystyle z(t)=\frac{\tilde{\zeta}}{\zeta^{1/2}}t+\frac{(\tilde{\zeta}-h_{0}\zeta^{1/2})}{2\zeta^{3/2}}t(t^{2}+\zeta)+\order{(t^2+\zeta)^2},

(81)

therefore
\arctan{(z/\tilde\zeta)}\sim\arctan{\big(t/\zeta^{1/2}\big)}
, one can expand
\delta_{\chi}(e,z)
as

\displaystyle\delta_{\chi}(e,z)=2\arctan{\big(t/\zeta^{1/2}\big)}+t\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(t^{2}+\zeta)^{j}\Delta_{\chi,j}(e)

\displaystyle\quad=2\arctan{\big(t/\zeta^{1/2}\big)}-\frac{t}{\zeta^{1/2}}\ln{\bigg(\frac{h_0(h_0^2+2\zeta^{1/2})}{2}\bigg)}-t(t^{2}+\zeta)\bigg[\frac{1}{2\zeta^{3/2}}\ln{\bigg(\frac{h_0(h_0^2+2\zeta^{1/2})}{2}\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2h_{0}\zeta^{1/2}(3+\beta_{e}^{2})(1+\beta_{e}^{2})^{2}-h_{0}^{3}(5-\beta_{e}^{2})(1-\beta_{e}^{2})^{2}-8(1-\beta_{e}^{4})}{12h_{0}^{2}(1-\beta_{e}^{2})^{2}\zeta}\bigg]+\order{(t^2+\zeta)^2}

(82)

where
\Delta_{\chi,j}(e)
are analytic functions among
0\leq e\leq 1
up to
\order{(t^2+\zeta^2)^2}
.
The leading term
\arctan{(t/\zeta^{1/2})}
cannot be expanded anymore, so we define 4 new integrals here,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{j,k}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}(s^{2}+y)^{j}\Big(\mathrm{arctan}\big(y^{1/2}/s\big)\Big)^{k}\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(83)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j,k}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}s(s^{2}+y)^{j}\Big(\mathrm{arctan}\big(y^{1/2}/s\big)\Big)^{k}\cos{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(84)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{j,k}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}(s^{2}+y)^{j}\Big(\mathrm{arctan}\big(y^{1/2}/s\big)\Big)^{k}\sin{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(85)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j,k}(y):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}s(s^{2}+y)^{j}\Big(\mathrm{arctan}\big(y^{1/2}/s\big)\Big)^{k}\sin{(s^3/3+ys)}\differential{s},

(86)

Obviously,
\mathcal{V}_{j,0}(y)=V_{j}(y),\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{0,0}(y)=\mathrm{Ai}^{\prime}(y),\mathcal{W}_{0,0}(y)=\mathrm{Gi}(y),\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{0,0}(y)=-\mathrm{Gi}^{\prime}(y)
. The tilde integrals
(\tilde{\mathcal{V}},\tilde{\mathcal{W}})
are equivalent to the derivative of
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W})
via,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j,k}(y)=-y^{1/2}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j,k}(y)+jy^{1/2}\mathcal{V}_{j-1,k}(y)+\frac{k}{2y^{1/2}}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j-1,k-1}(y),

(88)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{j,k}(y)=y^{1/2}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j,k}(y)+jy^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{j-1,k}(y)+\frac{k}{2y^{1/2}}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j-1,k-1}(y).

(89)

For the sake of simplicity, we keep the tilde integrals.
These integrals also satisfy some recurrences relations,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{j+1,k}(y)-ky^{1/2}\mathcal{W}_{j-1,k-1}(y)+2j\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j-1,k}(y)=0,

(90)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j+1,k}(y)-2jy\mathcal{W}_{j-1,k}(y)+(2j+1)\mathcal{W}_{j,k}(y)-ky^{1/2}\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j-1,k-1}(y)=0,

(91)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{j+1,k}(y)-2j\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j-1,k}(y)+ky^{1/2}\mathcal{V}_{j-1,k-1}=y^{j}\bigg(\frac{\pi}{2}\bigg)^{k},

(92)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{j+1,k}(y)+2jy\mathcal{V}_{j-1,k}(y)-(2j+1)\mathcal{V}_{j,k}(y)+ky^{1/2}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{j-1,k-1}(y)=0.

(93)

At last only
\big(\mathcal{V}_{0,k}(y),\mathcal{V}_{-1,k}(y),\mathcal{W}_{0,k}(y),\mathcal{W}_{-1,k}(y)\big)
and
\big(\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{0,k}(y),\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{-1,k}(y),\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{0,k}(y),\tilde{\mathcal{W}}_{-1,k}(y)\big)
would remain.
We show the results in Appendix
B.3
.

III.3
Cross-check of the two methods

So far, we have, in a rather straightforward manner, obtained formal asymptotic expansions of these integrals in the two limits
e\to 1
and
p\to\infty
.
However, several issues remain to be addressed:

1.

Although we have derived explicit mathematical expressions, they are composed of many non-trivial integrals, which still limits their direct applicability in practice.

2.

What is the precise relationship between these two kinds of asymptotic expansions?

This can be interpreted as an asymptotic expansion in two parameters,
\Delta_{e}
and
1/p^{1/3}
. The two approaches yield

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\Delta_{e}^{\alpha}\Big(c_{0}(p)+c_{1}(p)\Delta_{e}+c_{2}(p)\Delta_{e}^{2}+\order{\Delta_e^3}\Big)

\displaystyle=p^{\beta}\Big(d_{0}(e,y)+p^{-1/3}d_{1}(e,y)+p^{-2/3}d_{2}(e,y)+\order{p^{-1}}\Big).

(94)

Therefore, by performing a secondary expansion of the coefficients
c_{k}(p)
and
d_{k}(e,y\equiv p^{2/3}\zeta)
in the limits
p\to\infty
and
\Delta_{e}\to 0
, respectively, and reorganizing the result into a double series in
\Delta_{e}^{a}/p^{b/3}
, the coefficients must match term by term.
To achieve this, we again employ the matched asymptotic expansion method introduced in Section
III.1
on both
c_{k}(p)
and
d_{k}(e,y)
.

We first discuss how to expand
c_{k}(p)
into a power series in
1/p^{1/3}
. One can perform the same parameter transformation as (
55
),
(z-\sin{z})=t^{3}/3
, which rewrites the integral in
c_{k}(p)
in the following form,

\displaystyle I_{p}=\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}\bigg(\sin{(pt^3/3)}A_{S}(t)+\big(\cos{(pt^3/3)}-1\big)A_{C}(t)-B(p,t)\bigg)\differential{t}.

(95)

Since the integrand is analytic over the interval
0\leq z\leq\pi
, the transformed integrand is also analytic on
0\leq t\leq(3\pi)^{1/3}
.
However, when considered separately,
A_{S}(p,t)
,
A_{C}(p,t)
, and
B(t)
may be divergent; in other words, their Laurent expansions near
t\to 0
contain leading terms with negative powers.
One therefore can decompose the integral into five parts,

\displaystyle I_{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{s}}\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}\bigg(\frac{\hat{s}_{-k}\sin{(pt^3/3)}}{t^{k}}-\sum_{j}\frac{\bar{s}_{k,j}}{t^{\alpha_{k,j}}}\bigg)\differential{t}+\sum_{k=1}^{n_{c}}\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}\bigg(\frac{\hat{c}_{-k}\big(\cos{(pt^3/3)}-1\big)}{t^{k}}-\sum_{j}\frac{\bar{c}_{k,j}}{t^{\beta_{k,j}}}\bigg)\differential{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}\sin{(pt^3/3)}A_{S}^{\mathrm{reg}}(t)\differential{t}+\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}\big(\cos{(pt^3/3)}-1\big)A_{C}^{\mathrm{reg}}(t)\differential{t}-\int_{0}^{(3\pi)^{1/3}}B^{\mathrm{reg}}(p,t)\differential{t},

(96)

where we assume
A_{S}(t)
and
A_{C}(t)
admits the following Laurrent series

\displaystyle A_{S}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{s}}t^{-k}\hat{s}_{-k}+A_{S}^{\mathrm{reg}}(t),

(97)

\displaystyle A_{C}(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{c}}t^{-k}\hat{c}_{-k}+A_{C}^{\mathrm{reg}}(t),

(98)

The series
\sum_{j}(\bar{s}_{k,j})/t^{\alpha_{k,j}}
and
\sum_{j}(\bar{c}_{k,j})/t^{\beta_{k,j}}
represent the singular parts of

\hat{s}_{-k}\sin{(pt^3/3)}/t^{k}
and
\hat{c}_{-k}\big(\cos{(pt^3/3)}-1\big)/t^{k}
, respectively.
In other words, the first two integrals can be interpreted as the regular parts
18
of the integrals of
\hat{s}_{-k}\sin{(pt^3/3)}/t^{k}
and
\hat{c}_{-k}\big(\cos{(pt^3/3)}-1\big)/t^{k}
.
Accordingly,
B^{\mathrm{reg}}(p,t)
is defined as

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{reg}}(p,t):=B(p,t)-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{s}}\sum_{j}\frac{\bar{s}_{k,j}}{t^{\alpha_{k,j}}}-\sum_{k=1}^{n_{c}}\sum_{j}\frac{\bar{c}_{k,j}}{t^{\beta_{k,j}}}.

(99)

After this decomposition, the integrands of all resulting integrals become regular.

In this way, we can obtain the asymptotic expansions of all coefficients of
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}
in the limit
p\to\infty
. Here we present the results for the case
b=0
, while the coefficients for
b>0
are shown Appendix
A
.

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,0)}=1+\hat{p}^{1/3}\Delta_{e}\bigg(-\frac{\Gamma(2/3)}{\pi}+\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{10\pi}\hat{p}^{-1/3}-\frac{23\Gamma(2/3)}{4200\pi}\hat{p}^{-4/3}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+

\displaystyle\qquad+\hat{p}\Delta_{e}^{3}\bigg(-\frac{2}{3^{5/2}}-\frac{3\Gamma(2/3)}{10\pi}\hat{p}^{-2/3}+\frac{51\Gamma(1/3)}{1400\pi}\hat{p}^{-4/3}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(100)

\displaystyle\Delta^{3}_{e}\mathrm{J}_{(p,2,0)}=1+\hat{p}\Delta_{e}^{3}\bigg(-\frac{2}{3^{5/2}}-\frac{3\Gamma(2/3)}{10\pi}\hat{p}^{-2/3}+\frac{51\Gamma(1/3)}{1400\pi}\hat{p}^{-4/3}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(101)

where
\hat{p}:=\sqrt{3}p/2
. For
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,0)}
, the logarithmic term
\ln{(1-\cos{z})}
occurs, one can use

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}z}\ln{z}\differential{z}=\partialderivative{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{\alpha}\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}z}\differential{z}=\partialderivative{a}\Gamma(\alpha+1)\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\pi(\alpha+1)}.

(102)

The rest steps are the same, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}\mathrm{K}_{(p,1,0)}=\ln{2\Delta_e^2}+\hat{p}^{1/3}\Delta_{e}\bigg(\frac{1}{9\Gamma(1/3)}\big(2\sqrt{3}(-6+2\gamma_{E}+\ln{6})-4\pi\big)-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{180\pi}\big(9+12\gamma_{E}+4\sqrt{3}\pi+6\ln{6}\big)\hat{p}^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{9\sqrt{3}}{1400}\hat{p}^{-4/3}+\bigg(\frac{4}{3\sqrt{3}\Gamma(1/3)}-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{15\pi}\hat{p}^{-2/3}\bigg)\ln{p}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{e}^{2}+\hat{p}\Delta_{e}^{3}\bigg(\frac{1}{27\sqrt{3}}\big(-1+4\gamma_{E}-4\ln{3}+2\ln{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{3\Gamma(1/3)}\Big(\sqrt{3}\big(-7+4\gamma_{E}+2\ln{6}\big)-4\pi\Big)\hat{p}^{-2/3}-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{4200\pi}\big(34\sqrt{3}\pi+51\ln{6}+159+102\gamma_{E}\big)\hat{p}^{-4/3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg(\frac{4}{27\sqrt{3}}+\frac{2}{5\sqrt{3}\Gamma(1/3)}\hat{p}^{-2/3}-\frac{17\Gamma(1/3)}{700\pi}\hat{p}^{-4/3}\bigg)\ln{p}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{4}\Delta_{e}^{4}+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(103)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{3}\mathrm{K}_{(p,2,0)}=-1+\ln{2\Delta_e^2}+\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{2}_{e}+\hat{p}\Delta_{e}^{3}\bigg(\frac{2}{27\sqrt{3}}(-2+2\gamma_{E}-\ln{(9/2)})+\frac{1}{15\Gamma(1/3)}\Big(\sqrt{3}\big(-5+2\gamma_{E}+\ln{6}\big)-2\pi\Big)\hat{p}^{-2/3}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{8400\pi}(165+204\gamma_{E}+68\sqrt{3}\pi+102\ln{6})\hat{p}^{-4/3}+\bigg(\frac{4}{27\sqrt{3}}+\frac{2}{5\sqrt{3}\Gamma(1/3)}\hat{p}^{-2/3}-\frac{17\Gamma(1/3)}{700\pi}\hat{p}^{-4/3}\bigg)\ln{p}

\displaystyle\qquad+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg)+\frac{1}{4}\Delta_{e}^{4}+\order{\Delta_e^5}.

(104)

We can thus obtain the asymptotic expansions of these integrals in the joint limits
\Delta_{e}\to 0
and
p\to\infty
. In principle, the UAE method should yield consistent results. However, it is important to note that the UAE expansion is carried out under the condition
p\to\infty
, with
y=p^{2/3}\zeta
introduced as the uniform asymptotic variable, so that the expressions are organized in terms of a set of basic integrals.
For the regime of interest here, one must further derive the asymptotic expansions of these basic integrals when
y\to 0
.

Figure 1:
Comparison of the numerical results (labeled as ‘Numeric’) with the two approximation schemes—the
\Delta_{e}
asymptotic expansion (labeled as ‘AE’) and the uniform asymptotic expansion (labeled as ‘UAE’)—for several representative integrals in the range
e>0.5
. The selected examples are
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
,
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
, and
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(0,2),b=(1,2,3))}
. The upper panels correspond to the case
p=2
, while the lower panels show the case
p=10
.

Some of these integrals are directly related to
\mathrm{Ai}
and
\mathrm{Gi}
, such as
V_{0}(y)=\mathrm{Ai}(y)
and
\mathcal{W}_{0,0}(y)=\mathrm{Gi}(y)
, and can be easily calculated, while others need to be evaluated using the matched asymptotic expansion method introduced in Section.
III.1
.
We obtain

\displaystyle V_{-1}(y)=\frac{1}{2y^{1/2}}+\frac{\Gamma(-1/3)}{3^{5/6}2\pi}-\frac{1}{9}y-\frac{\Gamma(-5/3)}{3^{1/6}18\pi}y^{2}+\frac{1}{9}y^{5/2}+\order{y^3},

(105)

\displaystyle U_{0}(y)=-\frac{2\pi+\sqrt{3}(2\gamma_{E}+\ln{3})}{3^{2/3}\pi}\Gamma(4/3)+y^{1/2}+\frac{(-9+6\gamma_{E}-2\sqrt{3}\pi+3\ln{3})\Gamma(2/3)}{3^{5/6}6\pi}y+\frac{1}{4}y^{2}+\order{y^3},

(106)

\displaystyle U_{-1}(y)=\frac{\ln{y}+2\ln{2}}{2y^{1/2}}+\frac{140\Gamma(-10/3)(-18+7\gamma_{E}-2\sqrt{3}\pi+3\ln{3})}{3^{5/6}243\pi}+\frac{-1+4\gamma_{E}-4\ln{3}}{54}y

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{308\Gamma(-14/3)\big(20\pi+\sqrt{3}(3+20\gamma_{E}+10\ln{3})\big)}{3^{2/3}3645\pi}y^{2}+\bigg(-\frac{19}{135}+\frac{2}{9}\ln{2}+\frac{1}{9}\ln{y}\bigg)y^{5/2}+\order{y^3}.

(107)

Eventually, we obtain the asymptotic expansion of the auxiliary integrals appear in the UAE of
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0,1}(y)=\frac{3-\gamma_{E}+\ln{3}-\ln{y^{3/2}}}{3\pi}y^{1/2}+\frac{1}{3^{2/3}18}\bigg(-\frac{9}{\pi}\Gamma(1/3)+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\Gamma(2/3)}\bigg)y^{3/2}+\bigg(\frac{17}{3^{5/6}60\Gamma(1/3)}-\frac{\Gamma(2/3)}{3^{1/3}4\pi}\bigg)y^{5/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\order{y^{7/2}},

(108)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0,2}(y)=\ln{2}\,y^{1/2}-\frac{1}{3^{1/3}\Gamma(1/3)}y-\frac{7}{54}y^{4}+\bigg(-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{3^{1/6}4\pi}+\frac{127}{3^{2/3}450\Gamma(2/3)}\bigg)y^{3}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(109)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0,3}(y)=\bigg(\frac{3\pi\ln{2}}{4}-\frac{21\zeta_{R}(3)}{8\pi}\bigg)y^{1/2}-\frac{1}{3^{1/6}6\Gamma(2/3)}y^{3/2}-\frac{3^{1/6}}{4\Gamma(1/3)}y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}}

(110)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{-1,1}(y)=\frac{\pi}{8y^{1/2}}-\frac{y^{1/2}}{3^{1/3}6\Gamma(2/3)}-\frac{2}{3^{11/6}\Gamma(1/3)}y^{3/2}+\frac{1}{8100\pi}(-713+380\gamma_{E}+225\pi^{2}-380\ln{3}+1140\ln{y})y^{5/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\order{y^{7/2}},

(111)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{-1,2}(y)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{24y^{1/2}}-\frac{1}{18}y-\frac{1}{3^{5/3}\Gamma(2/3)}y^{2}+\frac{1}{1080}(41+10\pi^{2}-152\ln{2})y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(112)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{-1,3}(y)=\frac{\pi^{3}}{64y^{1/2}}-\frac{1}{3^{5/6}4\Gamma(1/3)}y^{3/2}+\frac{1}{4320\pi}(-2072+320\gamma_{E}+123\pi^{2}+15\pi^{4}-456\pi^{2}-320\ln{3}+960\ln{y}

\displaystyle\qquad+1596\zeta_{R}(3))y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(113)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0,1}(y)=\frac{y^{1/2}}{6}+\frac{1}{3^{2/3}6}\bigg(\frac{3\sqrt{3}\Gamma(1/3)}{\pi}-\frac{1}{\Gamma(2/3)}\bigg)y^{3}-\frac{5}{24}y^{4}+\bigg(\frac{17}{3^{1/3}60\Gamma(1/3)}-\frac{3^{1/6}\Gamma(2/3)}{4\pi}\bigg)y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(114)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0,2}(y)=\frac{1}{3^{5/3}\Gamma(1/3)}y+\frac{1}{432\pi}(512-112\gamma_{E}-45\pi^{2}+112\ln{3}-336\ln{y})y^{2}+\bigg(-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{3^{2/3}4\pi}+\frac{127}{3^{1/6}1350\Gamma(2/3)}\bigg)y^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\order{y^{7/2}},

(115)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0,3}(y)=\frac{1}{3^{2/3}2\Gamma(2/3)}y^{3/2}+\frac{1}{96}(-4-5\pi^{2}+112\ln{2})y^{2}-\frac{3^{2/3}}{4\Gamma(1/3)}y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{-1,0}(y)=\frac{1}{3^{5/6}\Gamma(1/3)}+\frac{-1-2\gamma_{E}+2\ln{3}-6\ln{y}}{9\pi}y+\bigg(-\frac{\Gamma(1/3)}{3^{2/3}4\pi}+\frac{2}{3^{1/6}15\Gamma(2/3)}\bigg)y^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\bigg(\frac{2}{3^{5/6}7\Gamma(1/3)}-\frac{\Gamma(2/3)}{3^{1/3}12\pi}\bigg)y^{3}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(116)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{-1,1}(y)=\frac{1}{3^{2/3}2\Gamma(2/3)}y^{1/2}+\frac{-1+4\ln{2}}{12}y-\frac{2}{3^{4/3}\Gamma(1/3)}y^{3/2}-\frac{19}{810}y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(117)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{-1,2}(y)=\frac{1}{72\pi}(44-8\gamma_{E}-3\pi^{2}+12\pi^{2}\ln{2}+8\ln{3}-8\ln{y^{3/2}}-24\zeta_{R}(3))y-\frac{1}{3^{13/6}\Gamma(2/3)}y^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{29}{3^{5/6}180\Gamma(1/3)}y^{3}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(118)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{-1,3}(y)=\frac{1}{48}\big(-\pi^{2}+4(6+\pi^{2})\ln{2}-18\zeta_{R}(3)\big)y-\frac{1}{3^{1/3}4\Gamma(1/3)}y^{3/2}-\frac{1}{27}y^{5/2}+\order{y^{7/2}},

(119)

where
\zeta_{R}
is the Riemann-zeta function. To distinguish it from the symbol
\zeta
, we add the subscript
R
.
The tilde integrals can be represented by their derivatives through Eq. (
88
) and (
89
).

Substituting these expansions into the UAE expressions obtained in Section
III.2
and then expanding in the limit
\Delta_{e}\to 0
, we find that the resulting coefficients of the
\Delta_{e}
expansion agree exactly with those obtained from the direct expansion, up to order
\order{p^{-4/3}}
.
In this way, we have systematically verified the asymptotic expansions of each integral.

To provide some intuitive insight, we present in FIG.
1
a comparison for several representative cases, namely
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
,
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
, and
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(0,2),b=(1,2,3))}
with
p=2
and
p=10
.
We compare the numerical results with those obtained from the two approximation methods.
It is observed that the UAE method provides consistently high accuracy overall, although the error becomes larger when
p
is small.
In contrast, the direct asymptotic expansion agrees well only in the regime
e\to 1
. These findings are consistent with expectations.
In practical applications, the UAE contains higher-order information in the
\Delta_{e}
expansion, whereas extending the direct expansion method becomes increasingly difficult.
Therefore, if one aims to obtain higher-order asymptotic information near the
e\to 1
boundary, the UAE approach is more suitable.

IV
Analytic approximation

Figure 2:
Comparison of the numerical results (labeled as ‘Numeric’) with the analytic approximated form of the PN-elliptic integrals (
123
) (labeled as ‘Approx’). The selected examples are
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
,
\mathrm{K}_{(p,a=(1,3),0)}
, and
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a=(0,2),b=(1,2,3))}
. The upper panels correspond to the case
p=10
, while the lower panels show the case
p=100
.

In the previous section, we derived the asymptotic expansions of these PN-elliptic integrals (
20
) and (
21
) in the two limits
e\to 0
and
e\to 1
.
Using this asymptotic information, we now attempt to construct an endpoint-constrained and accurate analytic approximation, which can significantly accelerate the computation.

Firstly we factorize the leading asymptotic behavior as

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}=\frac{e^{p}}{\Delta_{e}^{\mu_{a,b}}}\hat{J}_{(p,a,b)},

(120)

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}_{(p,a,b)}=\frac{e^{p}}{\Delta_{e}^{\mu_{a,b}}}\hat{K}_{(p,a,b)},

(121)

where

\displaystyle\mu_{a,b}=\begin{cases}2a-1&a>0\text{ and }b=0,2,\\
2a-4&a>2\text{ and }b=1,3.\end{cases}

(122)

The remaining parts,
\hat{J}
and
\hat{K}
, are then regular over
0\leq e\leq 1
after subtracting the potentially occurred logarithmic term.
We adopt the following form as an approximation for
\hat{I}=(\hat{J},\hat{K})
.
For simplicity, we will use
\hat{I}
to denote both integrals of
\hat{J}
and
\hat{K}
,

\displaystyle\hat{I}-\hat{d}^{\ln}_{0}\ln\Delta_{e}\approx\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sum_{n=0}^{N_{l}}c_{n}e^{2n}+e^{2N_{l}+2}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{N_{r}}d_{n}\Delta_{e}^{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{N_{r}}d_{n}^{\ln}\ln{\Delta_e}\,\Delta_{e}^{n}+\Delta_{e}^{N_{r}+1}\delta_{I}\bigg)&p<p_{0}\\
&\bigg[1+e^{2N_{l}+2}\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{N_{r}}d_{n}\Delta_{e}^{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{N_{r}}d_{n}^{\ln}\ln{\Delta_e}\,\Delta_{e}^{n}+\Delta_{e}^{N_{r}+1}\delta_{I}\bigg)\bigg]\exp\bigg( \sum_{n=0}^{N_l}c_ne^{2n} \bigg)&p\geq p_{0}.\end{aligned}\right.

(123)

Here the coefficients
c_{n}
are determined from the expansion of the integral in
e\to 0
, while
d_{n}
are fixed by the expansion
\Delta_{e}\to 0
.
The term
\delta_{I}
is a polynomial introduced to fit the remaining residual error.
And
\hat{d}^{\ln}_{0}\ln\Delta_{e}
represents a possible leading-order logarithmic contribution, where the coefficient
\hat{d}^{\ln}
is determined from the
\Delta_{e}\to 0
asymptotic expansion.

Figure 3:
Left panel: the error between the analytic approximation of the regularized integrals and the numerical results as a function of
p
.
Different types of integrals are distinguished by different colors. Right panel: the comparison of the worse fitted case
\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,3)}
, which corresponds to the most top line in the left panel, with the numeric result.

We find that these regularized integrals
\hat{I}
exhibit exponential decay in the limit
p\to\infty
.
The reason for this choice is that, in the limit
p\to\infty
, after factoring out the pre-factor
e^{p}
, Eqs. (
24
)–(
28
) indicate that the remaining part exhibits a leading-order growth approximately of the form
p^{p}
.
Accordingly, we introduce a threshold
p_{0}
, and for
p\geq p_{0}
we construct the approximation based on an exponential ansatz rather than continuing with a power series.
The parameters
(N_{l},N_{r},p_{0})
, as well as the order of the polynomial
\delta_{I}
, depend on the specific type of integral.
In most cases, we adopt
N_{l}=6
,
N_{r}=2
, and
p_{0}=10
and the order of
\delta_{I}
is 6 to 12, depends on the required accuracy.
Because the number of integrals involved is extremely large, and the detailed calculations are too cumbersome to present in full, we just show the main construction strategy here.

The key advantage of this approach is that it preserves the correct asymptotic behavior in both limits
e\to 0
and
e\to 1
, without mutual contamination between the two regimes.
The accuracy can then be systematically improved by increasing the order of the polynomial
\delta_{I}
.
After a series of involved calculations, we ultimately construct such endpoint-constrained analytic approximations for all integrals listed in Table
1
for
p\leq 200
.
Since the harmonics waveform
H_{p}\sim\order{e^p}
, this is sufficient for computations in the regime
e\lesssim 0.95
.
However, we must be clear that as the eccentricity approaches
e\to 1
, the number of required Fourier modes formally diverges.
As a result, the accumulated error from the approximate expressions also increases.
For this reason, a conservative choice is to restrict the use of this approximation to the regime
e\lesssim 0.9
.
In addition, for future modeling works, this method also provides the asymptotic expansions of
H_{\ell mp}
at two endpoints.
Therefore, the construction of the approximate model can be applied directly to
H_{\ell mp}
itself.
Since the explicit form of
H_{\ell mp}
depends on the choice of coordinates, we do not pursue this further here.

In FIG.
2
, we present two illustrative comparisons for
p=10
and
p=100
, respectively.
The agreement is visually indistinguishable. To quantify the accuracy we evaluate the error between the analytic approximations and the numerical results of
\hat{I}
for all cases and several worse fitted cases.
One should note that, since the regularized integrals
\hat{I}
span several orders of magnitude, neither the absolute error
\epsilon_{\mathrm{abs}}
nor the relative error
\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}}
alone provides an appropriate measure.
Instead, for a fixed
e
, we define the error as the smaller of the two,

\epsilon_{p}(e)=\min(\epsilon_{\mathrm{abs}},\epsilon_{\mathrm{rel}})
.
We then define the overall error level for a given
p
as

\epsilon_{p}:=\max\epsilon_{p}(e)

over the full range
0<e<1
.
In fact, apart from a few cases with
a<0
, where sign changes necessitate this treatment, for the vast majority of integrals
\epsilon_{p}
is actually the relative error.
The resulting dependence of
\epsilon_{p}
on
p
is shown in the left panel of FIG.
3
, where different colors indicate different types of integrals.
In the right panel of FIG.
3
, we show the case with the largest approximation error, namely
\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,3)}
, which corresponds to the highest curve in the error plot on the left panel.
We present two representative cases,
p=40
and
p=50
, and compare the absolute value on a logarithmic scale.
We find that the relatively large error in this case arises from the presence of a zero crossing, manifested as a small kink around
e\sim 0.9
in the figure, where the absolute error is about
5\times 10^{-4}
.
Currently, the errors are controlled below the level of
10^{-3}
.
If higher accuracy is required in the future, it can be systematically improved following the procedure outlined here.

Figure 4:
Comparison of the 3PN leading-mode waveform
h_{22}
.
In this example the binary has mass ratio
\nu=0.22
, initial frequency
v_{0}=0.17
, and the evolution ends at
v=1/\sqrt{6}
. The initial eccentricity is
e_{0}=0.7
.
The gray line corresponds to the numeric results, obtained by summing
h_{22}\propto v^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}2(l-\lambda)}\sum_{p}\hat{H}_{2(-2)p}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}pl}
until the required accuracy is reached.
The blue and red lines correspond to the results obtained by computing each mode
\hat{H}_{2(-2)p}
using our approximate expressions (
123
), and then summing over
p
up to
p=10
(labeled by ‘Approx
p_{\max}=10
’) and
p=30
(labeled by ‘Approx
p_{\max}=30
’), respectively.
The green line denotes the results of post-circular expansion truncated at
\order{e^{10}}
(labeled by ‘PC’).
The bottom panel shows the evolution of orbital eccentricity.

As a final example in this section, we present a concrete waveform calculation.
We consider a system with
e_{0}=0.7
and mass ratio
\nu=0.22
. The 3PN gravitational waveform
h_{22}
is computed and compared using three different methods:
(i) a fully numerical approach, in which each Fourier mode
\hat{H}_{2(-2)p}
is computed numerically and summed over
p
until the desired accuracy is reached;
(ii) a small-eccentricity expansion truncated at
\order{e^{10}}
;
(iii) the approach developed in this work, where the approximate integral expressions (
123
) are used to compute each Fourier mode
\hat{H}_{2(-2)p}
, with the sum over
p
truncated at
p_{\max}=10
and
p_{\max}=30
, respectively.

The comparison is shown in FIG (
4
).
We find that the approximate form possesses sufficient fitting capability.
Compared with the numerical computation, the cost of evaluating the approximation is essentially negligible, leading to a substantial acceleration in waveform generation.
More specifically, computing a single waveform point for one Fourier mode using numerical methods typically takes on the order of one second, whereas the analytic approximation requires only tens of nanoseconds.

On the other hand, the post-circular results perform significantly worse than the summation-based approach at the same number of summation.
This can be understood from the fact that the leading term in the small-eccentricity expansion of the
p
-th Fourier mode is of the order
\order{e^p}
.
The comparison shows that, even when summing over the same number of modes
p
, neglecting higher-order contributions still has a substantial impact, particularly at large eccentricities.
This highlights the necessity of accurately modeling each individual Fourier mode
H_{\ell(-m)p}
rather than relying solely on truncated small-eccentricity expansions.

V
Large eccentricity asymptotic expansion of eccentricity enhancement functions

In this section, we present another application of the UAE derived in Section
III.2
, namely the computation of the large-eccentricity asymptotic expansion of the eccentricity enhancement function.

The eccentricity enhancement functions (EEF) would appear during evaluating the orbit-averaged energy and angular momentum fluxes that contributed by tail parts.
Up to 3PN order and in MH coordinate, the tail-contributed, orbit-averaged energy flux
\left\langle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{tail}}}\right\rangle
and angular momentum flux
\left\langle{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tail}}}\right\rangle
in terms of time eccentricity
e\equiv e_{t}
are given by
10
;
11
;
12

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{tail}}}\right\rangle=\frac{32\pi\nu^{2}}{5}v^{13}\Bigg\{4\varphi(e)+v^{2}\Bigg[4\bigg(\varphi_{(0,1)}(e)-\frac{1}{2}\nu\varphi(e)-\frac{21}{1-e^{2}}\big(\varphi(e)-\varphi_{(1,0)}(e)\big)\bigg)+(1-4\nu)\bigg(\frac{16403}{2016}\beta(e)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{18}\gamma(e)\bigg)\Bigg]+v^{3}\bigg[\bigg(-\frac{515063}{11025}+\frac{16\pi^{2}}{3}-\frac{856}{35}\ln\left(\frac{v^{2}}{x_{0}^{\prime}}\right)\bigg)F(e)-\frac{1712}{105}\chi(e)\bigg]+\order{c^{-4}}\Bigg\},

(124)

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tail}}}\right\rangle=\frac{32\pi\nu^{2}}{5}v^{10}\Bigg\{4\tilde{\varphi}(e)+v^{2}\Bigg[4\bigg(\tilde{\varphi}_{(0,1)}(e)-\frac{1}{2}\nu\tilde{\varphi}(e)-\frac{18}{1-e^{2}}\big(\tilde{\varphi}(e)-\tilde{\varphi}_{(1,0)}(e)\big)\bigg)+(1-4\nu)\bigg(\frac{16403}{2016}\tilde{\beta}(e)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{18}\tilde{\gamma}(e)\bigg)\Bigg]+v^{3}\bigg[\bigg(-\frac{515063}{11025}+\frac{16\pi^{2}}{3}-\frac{856}{35}\ln\left(\frac{v^{2}}{x_{0}^{\prime}}\right)\bigg)\tilde{F}(e)-\frac{1712}{105}\tilde{\chi}(e)\bigg]+\order{c^{-4}}\Bigg\},

(125)

where
x^{\prime}_{0}
is constant
17
;
18
. The EEF is defined by summing the product of the multipole moments,

\displaystyle\varphi(e)=\frac{v^{8}}{32\nu^{2}}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{\varphi}(e)=-\mathrm{i}\frac{v^{8}}{16\nu^{2}}\epsilon_{zab}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ak}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{bk}^{*}\Big],

(126)

\displaystyle\varphi_{(a,b)}(e):=\frac{v^{8-2b}}{32\nu^{2}}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7-a}\mathrm{PN}_{b}\Big[\big[{}_{(a)}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}\big]\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{\varphi}_{(a,b)}(e):=-\mathrm{i}\frac{v^{8-2b}}{16\nu^{2}}\epsilon_{zij}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6-a}\mathrm{PN}_{b}\Big[\big[{}_{(a)}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ik}\big]\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{jk}^{*}\Big],

(127)

\displaystyle F(e)=\frac{v^{8}}{64\nu^{2}}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{8}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{F}(e)=-\mathrm{i}\frac{v^{8}}{32\nu^{2}}\epsilon_{zab}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ak}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{bk}^{*}\Big],

(128)

\displaystyle\chi(e)=\frac{v^{8}}{64\nu^{2}}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{8}\ln\bigg(\frac{p}{2}\bigg)\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{\chi}(e)=-\mathrm{i}\frac{v^{8}}{32\nu^{2}}\epsilon_{zab}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\ln\bigg(\frac{p}{2}\bigg)\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ak}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{bk}^{*}\Big],

(129)

\displaystyle\beta(e)=\frac{20v^{12}}{49209\nu^{2}(1-4\nu)}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{9}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ijk}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ijk}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{\beta}(e)=-\mathrm{i}\frac{20v^{12}}{16403\nu^{2}(1-4\nu)}\epsilon_{zab}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{8}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ajk}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{bjk}^{*}\Big],

(130)

\displaystyle\gamma(e)=\frac{4v^{6}}{\nu^{2}(1-4\nu)}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{7}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{S}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}\mathop{\mathrm{S}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ij}^{*}\Big],\qquad\tilde{\gamma}(e)=-\mathrm{i}\frac{8v^{6}}{\nu^{2}(1-4\nu)}\epsilon_{zab}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{6}\mathrm{PN}_{0}\Big[\mathop{\mathrm{S}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{ak}\mathop{\mathrm{S}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{bk}^{*}\Big],

(131)

where

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{L}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}\underset{(p-m,m)}{\mathrm{M}_{L}},

(132)

\displaystyle\big[{}_{(s)}\mathop{\mathrm{M}}\limits_{\mathclap{(p)}}\!{}_{L}\big]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m=-\ell}^{\ell}m^{s}\underset{(p-m,m)}{\mathrm{M}_{L}}.

(133)

The Fourier coefficients of multipole moments
\underset{(p,m)}{\mathrm{M}_{L}},\underset{(p,m)}{\mathrm{S}_{L}}
can be represented by Bessel functions and PN-elliptic integrals
51
, for example,

\displaystyle\underset{(p-2,2)}{\mathrm{M}_{xx}}=\frac{\nu}{v^{4}e^{2}p^{2}}\bigg[e\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\Big(1-\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p\Big)J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(2-e^{2}-2(1-e^{2})^{3/2}p\Big)J_{p}(ep)\bigg]+

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\nu}{84v^{2}e^{2}p^{3}(1-e^{2})}\Bigg\{J_{p}(ep)\bigg[(1-e^{2})\Big(4(e^{2}-1)^{2}(3\nu-1)p^{3}-2\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p^{2}(11e^{2}(\nu+2)+378\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+73\nu-113)

\displaystyle\quad+p(e^{2}(17\nu-57)+2(630\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+67\nu-195))-756\Big)+1008\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p+84(2p-9)\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad+2eJ^{\prime}_{p}(ep)\Bigg[\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\Big(\nu p(6(e^{2}-1)^{2}p^{2}-8\sqrt{1-e^{2}}e^{2}p-73\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p-25e^{2}+67)

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p\big(\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\big(p(2(e^{2}-1)p+23\sqrt{1-e^{2}}-378)-111\big)+90p+819\big)-756\Big)+315p\Bigg]\Bigg\}+

\displaystyle\quad+42p^{3}\Bigg[3\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,1)}(e)+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\bigg[]2p\mathrm{J}_{(p,-4,1)}(e)-2\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,0)}(e)+\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\Big((3-3\sqrt{1-e^{2}}p)\mathrm{J}_{(p,-3,1)}(e)

\displaystyle\quad+(2+3\sqrt{1-e^{2}})\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,0)}(e)-12\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)}(e)+6(1-e^{2})\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}(e)\Big)\bigg]\Bigg]+\order{c^{-4}},

(134)

Therefore the (Newtonian order) EEF can be represented by the summation of
(J_{p}(ep))^{2}
and its derivatives.
In practical waveform modeling, only the expansions in the limit
e\to 0
are typically used.
However, when the orbital eccentricity is large, the errors associated with neglecting the high-eccentricity behavior can accumulate in the phase.
It is therefore necessary to study the large-eccentricity regime.

In the following subsections we will show how to use our previous expansion to obtain the asymptotic expansion at large eccentricity of these EEF.

V.1
Asymptotic expansion of Newtonian order EEF

We define

\displaystyle X_{k}(e):=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{k}\big(J_{p}(ep)\big)^{2}.

(135)

When
k
is even,
X_{k}
admits a closed form, where the closed-form of
F(e)
and
\tilde{F}(e)
are well-known

\displaystyle F(e)=\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{13/2}}\bigg(1+\frac{85e^{2}}{6}+\frac{5171e^{4}}{192}+\frac{1751e^{6}}{192}+\frac{297e^{8}}{1024}\bigg),

(136)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(e)=\frac{1}{(1-e^{2})^{5}}\bigg(1+\frac{229e^{2}}{32}+\frac{327e^{4}}{64}+\frac{69e^{6}}{256}\bigg).

(137)

The rest Newtonian order EEF are given by

\displaystyle\varphi(e)=\frac{1-e^{2}}{8e^{2}}\left(e^{-1}X^{\prime}_{1}(e)+X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)\right)+\frac{1}{e^{2}}\left[(3-2e^{2})X_{3}(e)-\frac{3-5e^{2}+2e^{4}}{e}X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+\frac{1-2e^{2}+e^{4}}{8}X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)\right],

(138)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}(e)=\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{4e^{3}}\left[e^{2}X^{\prime}_{1}+2e(e^{2}-1)X^{\prime\prime}_{1}+2e(e^{2}-1)X_{3}+2(e^{4}-2e^{2}+1)X^{\prime}_{3}\right]=\varphi_{(1,0)}(e),

(139)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{(1,0)}(e)=\frac{1}{4e^{3}}\left(e(4-3e^{2})X_{1}+(-6+10e^{2}-4e^{4})X^{\prime}_{1}+2(1-e^{2})X^{\prime}_{-1}+2e(1-e^{2})^{2}X^{\prime\prime}_{1}+2e(1-e^{2})X^{\prime\prime}_{-1}\right),

(140)

\displaystyle\beta(e)=\frac{4}{16403e^{5}}\Big[30e^{9}X^{\prime\prime}_{5}(e)-150e^{8}X^{\prime}_{5}(e)-5e^{7}\big(37X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+24X^{\prime\prime}_{5}(e)+42X_{5}(e)\big)-5e^{6}\big(X^{\prime}_{3}(e)-156X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+2e^{5}\big(36X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+355X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+90X^{\prime\prime}_{5}(e)+60X_{3}(e)+360X_{5}(e)\big)+2e^{4}\big(36X^{\prime}_{1}(e)-95X^{\prime}_{3}(e)-720X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-15e^{3}\big(12X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+61X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+8X^{\prime\prime}_{5}(e)+24X_{3}(e)+54X_{5}(e)\big)-15e^{2}\big(12X^{\prime}_{1}(e)-35X^{\prime}_{3}(e)-76X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+120X^{\prime}_{1}(e)-330\big(X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)+30e\big(4X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+13X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+X^{\prime\prime}_{5}(e)+8X_{3}(e)+10X_{5}(e)\big)\Big],

(141)

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}(e)=\frac{4\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{16403e^{5}}\Big[180e^{8}X^{\prime}_{5}(e)+45e^{7}\big(7X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+4X_{5}(e)\big)-5e^{6}\big(89X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+144X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-30e^{5}\big(16X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+39X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+24X_{3}(e)+18X_{5}(e)\big)+e^{4}\big(-336X^{\prime}_{1}(e)+2290X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+1080X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+45e^{3}\big(32X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+31X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+48X_{3}(e)+12X_{5}(e)\big)+45e^{2}\big(16X^{\prime}_{1}(e)-81X^{\prime}_{3}(e)-16X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+180\big(-2X^{\prime}_{1}(e)+10X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+X^{\prime}_{5}(e)\big)-180e\big(6X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+3X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+8X_{3}(e)+X_{5}(e)\big)\Big],

(142)

\displaystyle\gamma(e)=\frac{1-e^{2}}{e}\Big(X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+eX^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)\Big),

(143)

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}(e)=\frac{2(1-e^{2})^{3/2}}{e}X^{\prime}_{3}(e),

(144)

\displaystyle\chi(e)=-(\ln{2})F(e)+\frac{1}{48e^{3}}\partialderivative{k}\Big[2e(3-2e^{2})X_{k-4}(e)-3(3-5e^{2})X^{\prime}_{k-4}(e)+3(1-e^{2})X^{\prime}_{k-6}(e)

\displaystyle\quad+3e(1-e^{2})^{2}X^{\prime\prime}_{k-4}(e)+3e(1-e^{2})X^{\prime\prime}_{k-6}(e)\Big]\bigg|_{k=8},

(145)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(e)=-(\ln{2})\tilde{F}(e)+\frac{8\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{e^{3}}\partialderivative{k}\Big[-2e(1-e^{2})X_{k-3}(e)+e^{2}X^{\prime}_{k-5}(e)-2e(1-e^{2})X^{\prime\prime}_{k-5}(e)\Big]\bigg|_{k=7}.

(146)

None of these functions have closed form.
In order to obtain the asymptotic behavior of
X_{k}(e)
at
e\to 1
, we can use the UAE of
J_{p}(ep)

\displaystyle J_{p}(ep)=\left(\frac{4\zeta}{1-e^{2}}\right)^{1/4}p^{-1/3}\mathrm{Ai}\left(p^{2/3}\zeta\right)+\order{p^{-5/3}},

(147)

The
X_{k}(e)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle X_{k}(e)=h_{0}^{2}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}p^{k}\bigg[p^{-2/3}\big(\mathrm{Ai}(p^{2/3}\zeta)\big)^{2}+\order{p^{-2}}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad:=h_{0}^{2}S\big[\mathrm{Ai}^{2}\big](-2/3;\zeta)+\order{p^{-2}},

(148)

where, we define

\displaystyle S[f](\alpha;\zeta)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{\alpha}f(p^{2/3}\zeta),

(149)

where the function
f(x)
is analytic as
x\to 0
.
If
\alpha>0
, then this summation will actually be dominated by the large
p
, which corresponds to the UAE case we are considering, that is,
p\to\infty,\zeta\to 0
.
However, when
\alpha<0
, the contribution of small
p
cannot be ignored.
To obtain the correct asymptotic expression, we should employ expansion

\displaystyle\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}p^{\alpha}f(p^{2/3}\zeta)=\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}p^{\alpha}f(0)+\zeta\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}p^{\alpha+2/3}f^{\prime}(0)+...=

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\alpha}}\frac{\zeta^{j}f^{(j)}(0)}{j!}\zeta_{R}\bigg(-\alpha-\frac{2}{3}j\bigg)+...

(150)

where
j_{\alpha}:=\lfloor-3(\alpha+1)/2\rfloor
.
The contribution of negative
\alpha
is given by the first
j_{\alpha}
expansion terms.
Thus we define

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\alpha}(x)=f(x)-\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\alpha}}\frac{x^{j}f^{(j)}(0)}{j!}.

(151)

The summation is rewritten as

\displaystyle S[f](\alpha;\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\alpha}}\frac{\zeta^{j}f^{(j)}(0)}{j!}\zeta_{R}\left(-\alpha-\frac{2}{3}j\right)+\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}p^{\alpha}\hat{f}_{\alpha}(p^{2/3}\zeta).

(152)

Now the summation is dominated by large
p
, which can be converted to integral through Euler–Maclaurin (EM) formula.

\displaystyle\sum_{k=m}^{n}f(k)=\int_{m}^{n}f(x)\differential{x}+\frac{1}{2}\left(f(n)+f(m)\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{\left[\lambda/2\right]}\frac{B_{2j}}{(2j)!}\left(f^{(2j-1)}(n)-f^{(2j-1)}(m)\right)+R_{\lambda},

(153)

where
B_{j}
is the
j
th Bernoulli number and the remainder term
R_{\lambda}
can be estimated as

\displaystyle|R_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{2\zeta_{R}(\lambda)}{(2\pi)^{\lambda}}\int_{m}^{n}|f^{(\lambda)}(x)|\differential{x}.

(154)

We then substitute the UAE form of
J_{p}(ep)
to Euler–Maclaurin formula and carefully choose an integer
\lambda
that the remainder term
R_{\lambda}\sim\order{\zeta^{n+1}}
.

S[f](\alpha;\zeta)
in terms of integral reads

\displaystyle S[f](\alpha;\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\alpha}}\frac{\zeta^{j}f^{(j)}(0)}{j!}\zeta_{R}\left(-\alpha-\frac{2}{3}j\right)+\frac{3}{2}\zeta^{-3(\alpha+1)/2}I[f]\left(\frac{3\alpha+1}{2};\zeta\right)+\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}[f](\alpha;\zeta),

(155)

where
j_{\alpha}=\lfloor-3(\alpha+1)/2\rfloor-1
. If
j_{\alpha}<0
, the first summation term vanishes.

\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}[f](\alpha;\zeta)
denotes the boundary term and Bernoulli corrections,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\lambda}[f](\alpha;\zeta)=\frac{1}{2}f(\zeta)-\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor\lambda/2\rfloor}\frac{B_{2j}}{(2j)!}\derivative[2j-1]{p}\evaluated{\Big[p^\alpha\hat{f}(p^{2/3}\zeta)\Big]}_{p=1},

(156)

The integral
I[f]
is given by

\displaystyle I[f]\left(\frac{3\alpha+1}{2};\zeta\right)=\int_{\zeta}^{\infty}x^{(3\alpha+1)/2}\hat{f}_{\alpha}(x)\differential{x}.

(157)

Note that the lowest power of
x^{(3\alpha+1)/2}\hat{f}_{\alpha}(x)\sim x^{(3\alpha+1)/2+j_{\alpha}+1}+\order{x^{(3\alpha+1)/2+j_\alpha+2}}
. It may be equal to
-1
, in which case the logarithm term would appear. We define

\displaystyle I[f]\left(\frac{3\alpha+1}{2};\zeta\right):=I[f]\left(\frac{3\alpha+1}{2}\right)-\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{\hat{f}_{\alpha}^{(j)}(0)}{j!}\left[\frac{(1-\delta_{(3\alpha+1)/2+j+1})}{(3\alpha+1)/2+j+1}\zeta^{(3\alpha+1)/2+j+1}+\delta_{(3\alpha+1)/2+j+1}\ln\zeta\right],

(158)

The integral
I[f]\big((3\alpha+1)/2\big):=\mathop{\mathrm{FP}}\limits_{\zeta\to 0}I[f]\big((3\alpha+1)/2;\zeta\big)
denotes removing the diverge part of the integrand as
\zeta\to 0
.
Here we only retain up to
\order{\Delta_e^2}
, so higher-order corrections will not actually occur, thus we can obtain UAE of
X_{k}(e)
when
k>0

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{3k+1}X_{k}(e)=-\frac{3}{5}2^{k}\big(-5+3k\Delta_{e}^{2}\big)I[\mathrm{Ai}^{2}]\bigg(\frac{3k-1}{2}\bigg)+\order{\Delta_e^4}

(159)

Figure 5:

Comparison between the numerical results for the regularized EEFs (black cross markers)
(\varphi,\tilde{\varphi},\beta,\tilde{\beta},\gamma,\tilde{\gamma},\chi,\tilde{\chi})
and the asymptotic expansion (red solid line) in the limit
\Delta_{e}\to 1
.

Specially, when
k=-1
,

\displaystyle X_{-1}(e)

\displaystyle=\frac{6^{2/3}2450\Gamma^{2}(1/3)\zeta_{R}(5/3)-280\sqrt{3}\pi\zeta_{R}(3)+6^{1/3}3\Gamma^{2}(2/3)\zeta(13/3)}{29400\pi^{2}}+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{2646000\pi^{2}}\Big[6^{2/3}44100\Gamma(1/3)\zeta_{R}(5/3)

\displaystyle\quad+35\sqrt{3}\pi\big(-52285+25200\gamma_{E}-12600\ln{(4/3)}-368\zeta_{R}(e)\big)+6^{4/3}\Gamma^{2}(2/3)\big(3150\zeta_{R}(7/3)+37\zeta_{R}(13/3)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sqrt{3}\Delta_{e}^{2}}{\pi}\ln\Delta_{e}+\order{\Delta_e^4}.

(160)

Substituting these to the EEFs, we find

(161)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{7}\tilde{\varphi}(e)=\frac{120-103\Delta_{e}^{2}}{5\pi\sqrt{3}}+\order{\Delta_e^4},

(162)

(163)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{9}\tilde{\beta}(e)=\frac{2560(1315-1737\Delta_{e}^{2})}{49209\pi\sqrt{3}}+\order{\Delta_e^4},

(164)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{12}\gamma(e)=\frac{64(30-61\Delta_{e}^{2})}{3\pi\sqrt{3}}+\order{\Delta_e^4},

(165)

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{9}\tilde{\gamma}(e)=\frac{64(25-36\Delta_{e}^{2})}{15\pi\sqrt{3}}+\order{\Delta_e^4}.

V.2
EEF at 1PN order

At 1PN order,
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}(e)
would appear. But we can always express the EEF at 1PN using only
J_{p}(ep),\mathrm{J}_{{(p,a,b)}}
and
J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)
.
Here we define

\displaystyle X_{k}(a,b,e)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{k}J_{p}(ep)\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}(e),

(170)

\displaystyle Y_{k}(a,b,e)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}p^{k}\big(J^{\prime}_{p}(ep)\big)\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}(e).

(171)

We express EEF terms
\varphi_{(0,1)}(e)
and
\tilde{\varphi}_{(0,1)}(e)
by
X_{k}
and
Y_{k}
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{(0,1)}(e)=\frac{1}{252e^{4}(-1+e^{2})}\Big[e^{6}(390-92\nu)-3e^{2}(1092\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+67\nu-111)-252\ \sqrt{1-e^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+e^{4}(2646\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+293\nu-891)\Big]X_{3}(e)+\frac{(1-e^{2})^{2}}{84e^{4}}\Big[e^{2}(6\nu-2)-21(3\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+2)\Big]X_{5}(e)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{168e^{3}}\bigg[e^{2}\bigg(\frac{819}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}+25\nu-111\bigg)-\frac{378}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}-67\nu+111\bigg]X^{\prime}_{1}(e)-\frac{1}{336e^{3}}\Big[-6e^{4}(\nu-26)

\displaystyle\quad+e^{2}(3213\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+328\nu-968)-6(910\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+67\nu-111)\Big]X^{\prime}_{3}(e)+\frac{(e^{2}-1)^{3}(3\nu-1)}{42e^{3}}X^{\prime}_{5}(e)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{168}\bigg[\frac{1}{e^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1890}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}+67\nu-111\bigg)-\frac{1575}{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}-25\nu+111\bigg]X^{\prime\prime}_{1}(e)+\frac{1-e^{2}}{168e^{2}}\Big[e^{2}(2\nu+25)

\displaystyle\quad+441\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+79\nu-73\Big]X^{\prime\prime}_{3}(e)+\frac{2-e^{2}}{4e^{4}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}X_{6}(-4,1,e)+\frac{9\sqrt{1-e^{2}}(e^{2}-2)}{8e^{4}}X_{6}(-3,1,e)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3(1-e^{2})^{3/2}}{e^{4}}X_{6}(-2,1,e)-\frac{3(1-e^{2})^{5/2}}{2e^{4}}X_{6}(-1,1,e)-\frac{3(e^{2}-2)}{4e^{3}\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}Y_{5}(-3,1,e)-\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{2e^{3}}Y_{7}(-4,1,e)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{(0,1)}(e)=-\frac{9(3e^{2}-4)}{2e^{2}(e^{2}-1)}X_{1}(e)-\frac{\sqrt{1-e^{2}}}{84e^{4}}\Big[e^{4}(303-62\nu)+2e^{2}(441\sqrt{1-e^{2}}+73\nu-71)+42(\sqrt{1-e^{2}}-2)\Big]X_{3}(e)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{9}{e^{3}}\Big(X^{\prime}_{-1}(e)+eX^{\prime\prime}_{-1}(e)\Big)-\frac{1}{168e^{3}(1-e^{2})}\Big[(1-e^{2})^{5/2}(26\nu-214)+14(1-e^{2})^{3/2}(2\nu+33)-42\sqrt{1-e^{2}}(\nu+4)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{3(e^{2}-2)^{2}}{4e^{4}(e^{2}-1)}X_{4}(-3,1,e)+\frac{3(e^{2}-2)}{e^{4}}X_{4}(-2,1,e)+\frac{3(e^{4}-3e^{2}+2)}{2e^{4}}X_{4}(-1,1,e)+\frac{e^{2}-1}{e^{4}}X_{6}(-4,1,e)

To study the asymptotic properties of these EEF appear in 1PN or even higher-order post-Newtonian fluxes, one way is to imitate the UAE of the Bessel functions and the elliptic integrals
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,1)}(e)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-4,1)}=-\frac{12\pi\zeta}{h_{0}^{3}p^{7/3}}\mathrm{Gi}(p^{2/3}\zeta)+\frac{24\zeta^{2}}{h_{0}^{3}p^{7/3}}\mathcal{W}_{0,1}(p^{2/3}\zeta)+\order{p^{-8/3}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}=-\frac{h_{0}\pi}{p^{1/3}}\mathrm{Gi}(p^{2/3}\zeta)+\frac{2h_{0}}{p^{1/3}}\mathcal{W}_{0,1}(p^{2/3}\zeta)+\order{p^{-2/3}}

Fortunately, the
k

\displaystyle 4\bigg(\varphi_{(0,1)}(e)-\frac{1}{2}\nu\varphi(e)-\frac{21}{1-e^{2}}\big(\varphi(e)-\varphi_{(1,0)}(e)\big)\bigg)=-\frac{428}{21}\alpha(e)+\frac{178}{21}\nu\theta(e),

\displaystyle 4\bigg(\tilde{\varphi}_{(0,1)}(e)-\frac{1}{2}\nu\tilde{\varphi}(e)-\frac{18}{1-e^{2}}\big(\tilde{\varphi}(e)-\tilde{\varphi}_{(1,0)}(e)\big)\bigg)=-\frac{428}{21}\tilde{\alpha}(e)+\frac{178}{21}\nu\tilde{\theta}(e).

It is expected the summation like

These results can provide useful reference for future resummation and phenomenological calibration in waveform modeling.

Comparison between the numerical results for the EEFs (black cross markers)

VI
Summary

With the continuous advancement of GW detection in recent years, data analysis increasingly demands higher accuracy and more refined parameter dependence in waveform templates.
In this work, we have presented two asymptotic methods for computing the Fourier modes of post-Newtonian binary waveforms in the high-eccentricity regime.
Based on these developments, we have demonstrated two main applications.
First, by incorporating new endpoint asymptotic information, we construct an endpoint-constrained analytic approximation that significantly accelerate waveform generation, while also providing practical foundation for future construction of high-eccentricity frequency-domain post-Newtonian templates.
Second, we derive the large-eccentricity asymptotic expansion of the eccentricity enhancement function, which is otherwise difficult to compute, thereby contributing to more accurate template modeling.

Nevertheless, frequency-domain GW modeling still faces several challenges.
First, the dynamical parameters used in frequency-domain models differ from those in time-domain formulations.
In particular, frequency-domain approaches require the inclusion of post-adiabatic corrections to properly account for radiation-reaction effects, and the discrepancy between the two approaches becomes more pronounced as the binary approaches merger.
Moreover, the frequency-domain framework relies heavily on analytic Fourier transforms, typically implemented through approximations such as the SPA and SUA.
For eccentric waveforms, this leads to expressions written as sums over Fourier modes
\hat{H}_{p}
.
Although the present work provides a substantial acceleration in the computation of these modes, the number of required modes grows rapidly
e\to 1
, still causing the computation impractical in this case.

One may envision a smooth transition in the frequency-domain waveform description from bound systems (
e<1
) to scattering systems (
e>1
), but existing methods are clearly insufficient to achieve this.
These considerations indicate that the construction of frequency-domain GW templates in the high-eccentricity regime, as well as the development of a consistent framework bridging bound and scattering dynamics, will likely require fundamentally new approaches in future work.

Appendix A
Asymptotic expansion of PN elliptic integrals

Here we show the asymptotic expansion results of all elliptic integrals
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,b)}
that
b>0
.
They are expanded in terms of
\Delta_{e}
.
Up to
\order{\Delta_e^4}
, the expansion reads
For
\mathrm{J}_{(p,a,1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-2,1)}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)(1-\cos{z})^{2}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}+\frac{\Delta_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(1-\cos{z})\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)(1-\cos{z})\big[4p\sin{z}(\cos{z}-1)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}-8\cos{z}\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}\big]\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{3}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(p(1-\cos{z})^{2}(1+\cos{z})\cos{p(z-\sin{z})}+\frac{1}{3}(1+4\cos{z})\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\bigg)\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{4}}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)\Big[\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}\big(p^{2}(1-\cos{z})\cos^{2}{z}(\cos{z}+1)+p^{2}(1-\cos{z})(\cos{z}+1)

\displaystyle\qquad-2\cos{z}\left(p^{2}(1-\cos{z})(\cos{z}+1)+1\right)\big)+p\sin{z}\left(3\cos^{2}{z}-2\cos{z}-1\right)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}\Big]\differential{z}+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(189)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,-1,1)}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)(1-\cos{z})\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}+\frac{\Delta_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin{z}\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)\big(p\sin{z}(\cos{z}-1)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}-\cos{z}\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}\big)\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\Delta_{e}^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}p\left(1-\cos^{2}{z}\right)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}+\frac{\sin{z}(\cos{z}+1)\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}}{6(1-\cos{z})}\bigg)\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\Delta_{e}^{4}}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)\Big[\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}\left(-\left(p^{2}(1-\cos{z})(\cos{z}+1)\right)+p^{2}(1-\cos{z})\cos{z}(\cos{z}+1)+\cos{z}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+p\sin{z}(\cos{z}+1)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}\Big]\differential{z}+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(190)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,0,1)}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}(\pi-z)\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}-\frac{\Delta_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\sin{z}\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}}{\cos{z}-1}\bigg)\differential{z}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\Delta_{e}^{2}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}p(\pi-z)\sin{z}\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}\differential{z}-\frac{\Delta_{e}^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(-\frac{1}{2}p(\cos{z}+1)\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\sin{z}(2\cos{z}-1)\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}}{6(\cos{z}-1)^{2}}\bigg)\differential{z}+\Delta_{e}^{4}\Bigg[-\frac{5p}{24}+\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}p(\pi-z)\sin{z}\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}

\displaystyle\qquad-p\sin{z}\sin{p (z-\sin(z))}\Bigg]+\order{\Delta_e^5},

(191)

\displaystyle\mathrm{J}_{(p,1,1)}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{(\pi-z)\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}}{1-\cos{z}}\differential{z}+\frac{\Delta_{e}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin{z}\sin{p(z-\sin{z})}}{(1-\cos{z})^{2}}\differential{z}+\Delta_{e}^{2}\Bigg[-\frac{p}{6}+\frac{2p}{3}\ln{\frac{2\Delta_e}{\pi}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\bigg(-\frac{p(\pi-z)\sin{z}\cos{p (z-\sin{z})}}{2(1-\cos{z})}+\frac{(\pi-z)\cos{z}\sin{p (z-\sin{z})}}{2(1-\cos{z})^{2}}+\frac{2p\pi}{3z}\bigg)\differential{z}\Bigg]+\Delta_{e}^{3}
